I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.

Sometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.

So… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.

Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?

I have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.

I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.

Maybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.

She was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.

But I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.

I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.

I mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.

I mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.

I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.

Lately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.

Even my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.

I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.

I had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.

And I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.

I’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.

Once I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. But I do keep going back over everything with my sister, those last weeks, and thinking I should have called more. Or listened better. Or done something actually useful.

That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m talking slower… though I suppose I haven’t been talking to that many people lately.\n\nMoving slower—maybe a little? But then I’m 69, so sometimes I wonder what’s just normal creaking and what’s… something else. My sister used to tease me because I’d take forever choosing tomatoes at the store, but that was always me, even before all this.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. But I do keep going back over everything with my sister, those last weeks, and thinking I should have called more. Or listened better. Or done something actually useful.\n\nShe always hated fuss, you know, she’d say, “Don’t hover,” and then I think maybe I took that too literally. I’ll be washing a cup or looking at the phone and suddenly I’m thinking about some afternoon I didn’t call because I was… I don’t even know, sorting coupons or something pointless. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but I still keep telling myself I should have done more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. Even little things seem to take it out of me now. Like running a simple errand, or getting the papers together for the Cultural Center—those shouldn’t be big tasks, but afterward I feel completely worn out.

It’s hard to explain… it’s like my body is moving through thick mud. I’ll start sorting papers and then notice I’m just sitting there holding the same folder. And then I think, well, maybe I should reorganize the whole drawer while I’m at it, because there are receipts from years ago in there, and my sister always kept her paperwork so neat—

But yes. Tired. Low energy. More than usual, I think.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m talking slower… though I suppose I haven’t been talking to that many people lately.\n\nMoving slower—maybe a little? But then I’m 69, so sometimes I wonder what’s just normal creaking and what’s… something else. My sister used to tease me because I’d take forever choosing tomatoes at the store, but that was always me, even before all this.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. More than usual, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.20, F33.9, F41.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all. I don’t mean I have some plan to do anything to myself. I don’t. It just… scared me hearing that thought in my own head.\n\nAnd I almost didn’t make the appointment, you know, because I kept thinking, “Oh, everyone has bad mornings,” and then I got distracted by the house and the laundry and all that. But it kept coming back, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about a month now. It started after my sister died unexpectedly. I keep thinking I should know the exact day, but everything around that time is sort of a blur—there were calls to make, people asking about arrangements, and you know how families can be… but yes, about a month.\n\nDoctor: For most of the day lately, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel low, sure, but “most of the day” sounds so definite. Some days I keep myself busy with little things around the house, and then suddenly it’s evening and I realize I haven’t really checked in with myself at all.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? I’m not sure I’d say I’m down all day, every day. It sort of comes and goes, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister died, have you found yourself intensely longing for her or yearning to have her back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, of course I miss her, but “intensely longing” sounds… I don’t know, like something very sharp and constant.\n\nSometimes I think of calling her, and then I remember—well. But then I’ll get sidetracked by something silly, like I found an old recipe card the other day and spent twenty minutes trying to remember whose handwriting it was. So I don’t know. I miss her, yes, but I’m not sure if it’s what you mean by yearning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, is it mostly because of your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it’s certainly around that time, so it feels connected, but I don’t always have a neat reason when it happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll just be making tea or folding a towel and I get this low feeling, and then I start wondering if I was thinking about her or if it was just… there already. I’ve never been very good at sorting feelings into boxes. My sister used to be much better at that sort of thing—well, not feelings maybe, but organizing things. She had labels on everything in her pantry.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s mostly because of her death. It might be, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still go to things, you know, like book club, and there’s that dance night I’ve always liked—though I’m not much of a dancer, really, more of a “stand near the snacks and chat” person.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of… behind glass. Everyone else is laughing or talking about the book, and I’m nodding along, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nEven the garden, and cooking for the neighbors—those used to give me a little lift. Now I still do them, but it’s more like I’m just doing the motions.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That’s a big word, isn’t it?\n\nI have moments where things feel very flat, like I can’t quite imagine enjoying much, but then I’ll still think, well, I should water the geraniums before they get dramatic. They do this wilting thing where you’d think they’ve been abandoned for weeks, when really it’s just one warm afternoon.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say hopeless. Maybe discouraged sometimes? Or just tired of how things feel. But hopeless… I don’t know that I can quite say that.\n\nDoctor: If your sister had not died when she did, do you think you would still be feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know. I’ve gone over that in my head, actually—more than is probably useful. Like when you misplace your glasses and keep checking the same table as if they’ll appear the third time.\n\nMaybe I would feel different. Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m talking slower… though I suppose I haven’t been talking to that many people lately.\n\nMoving slower—maybe a little? But then I’m 69, so sometimes I wonder what’s just normal creaking and what’s… something else. My sister used to tease me because I’d take forever choosing tomatoes at the store, but that was always me, even before all this.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. But I do keep going back over everything with my sister, those last weeks, and thinking I should have called more. Or listened better. Or done something actually useful.\n\nShe always hated fuss, you know, she’d say, “Don’t hover,” and then I think maybe I took that too literally. I’ll be washing a cup or looking at the phone and suddenly I’m thinking about some afternoon I didn’t call because I was… I don’t even know, sorting coupons or something pointless. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but I still keep telling myself I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Even little things seem to take it out of me now. Like running a simple errand, or getting the papers together for the Cultural Center—those shouldn’t be big tasks, but afterward I feel completely worn out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… it’s like my body is moving through thick mud. I’ll start sorting papers and then notice I’m just sitting there holding the same folder. And then I think, well, maybe I should reorganize the whole drawer while I’m at it, because there are receipts from years ago in there, and my sister always kept her paperwork so neat—\n\nBut yes. Tired. Low energy. More than usual, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I’ll sit down with my budgeting spreadsheet—just the usual household things, nothing complicated—and I’ll find myself staring at the same line like it’s written in another language.\n\nOr I’ll be reading a paragraph for the workshop, and I have to reread it three or four times and still don’t really take in what it says. Then I start thinking maybe I need better lighting, or maybe it’s my glasses, because I’ve been meaning to get those checked anyway… but no, I think it’s more that my mind just won’t stay with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
