Or maybe something else would have brought me low eventually. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nShe was such a presence, you know. She used to call me about the most ordinary things—whether her soup needed more salt, or if a sweater looked “too beige,” which always made me laugh because half her closet was beige. So when I think of her not dying then, I imagine all those little calls still happening, and then I think maybe I wouldn’t feel quite like this.\n\nBut I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clean answer to that.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem connected to your sister’s death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are. It’s been about a month now, since she died so unexpectedly, and that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nI mean, I keep thinking about how strange time is—how a month can feel like three days and also like a year. I still catch myself wanting to tell her little things, like the neighbor’s dog got loose again and made a grand tour of the block like he was mayor. And then I remember.\n\nSo yes… I suppose it does feel connected to losing her.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever gone through a period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad stretches before, of course—who gets to 69 without some gray weather passing through? But whether it was like this… I don’t know. I’d have to think. Life has so many little seasons, doesn’t it? Some of them you don’t name while you’re in them.\n\nThere was a winter years ago when I remember making a lot of soup and not wanting to answer the phone, but then again, everyone was getting those nuisance calls about car warranties, so that may not prove much.\n\nSo… before my sister died, I can’t really say I remember a period quite like this clearly. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can point to a clear time like that before she died… not two weeks or longer nearly every day, at least not that I remember.\n\nLately, though, yes—I still make myself go to book club, and there’s that little dance night at the community center, which used to be fun even when the music was too loud. But now I’m there and it feels like I’m sitting behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves.\n\nEven my garden… I used to get such a lift from checking the tomatoes, or cooking something extra for the neighbors. Now I still do some of it, because plants don’t care if you’re in the mood, they just keep growing—but it doesn’t give me that same little spark it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been making it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I mean, I’m still managing the basics, but not the way I usually do.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, because I just couldn’t keep up with the preparation like I normally would. Usually I’m the one with all the little handouts and lists and backup pens—too many backup pens, probably. My sister used to tease me about that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than I used to. Not in a dramatic way, just… “not today,” more often than I like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. Or… not exactly less all in one piece. It’s more like I sleep in scraps.\n\nI’ll doze off for a while, then wake up, then maybe drift again. And then before dawn I’m just awake—my mind already off and running, like someone opened all the drawers in the kitchen at once. I used to like early mornings, actually. My mother was an early riser, always said the quiet was “free time nobody could spend for you.” But lately it doesn’t feel peaceful.\n\nOnce I’m up that early, I usually can’t settle back down before the day starts.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of enjoyment been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. It’s been about a month now, I think—since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nTime has gotten a little strange, honestly. I keep thinking it was just last week, and then I’ll remember all these little things that have happened since, like the mail piling up or someone bringing over a casserole dish I still haven’t returned. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m talking slower… though I suppose I haven’t been talking to that many people lately.\n\nMoving slower—maybe a little? But then I’m 69, so sometimes I wonder what’s just normal creaking and what’s… something else. My sister used to tease me because I’d take forever choosing tomatoes at the store, but that was always me, even before all this.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. But I do keep going back over everything with my sister, those last weeks, and thinking I should have called more. Or listened better. Or done something actually useful.\n\nShe always hated fuss, you know, she’d say, “Don’t hover,” and then I think maybe I took that too literally. I’ll be washing a cup or looking at the phone and suddenly I’m thinking about some afternoon I didn’t call because I was… I don’t even know, sorting coupons or something pointless. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but I still keep telling myself I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Even little things seem to take it out of me now. Like running a simple errand, or getting the papers together for the Cultural Center—those shouldn’t be big tasks, but afterward I feel completely worn out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… it’s like my body is moving through thick mud. I’ll start sorting papers and then notice I’m just sitting there holding the same folder. And then I think, well, maybe I should reorganize the whole drawer while I’m at it, because there are receipts from years ago in there, and my sister always kept her paperwork so neat—\n\nBut yes. Tired. Low energy. More than usual, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I’ll sit down with my budgeting spreadsheet—just the usual household things, nothing complicated—and I’ll find myself staring at the same line like it’s written in another language.\n\nOr I’ll be reading a paragraph for the workshop, and I have to reread it three or four times and still don’t really take in what it says. Then I start thinking maybe I need better lighting, or maybe it’s my glasses, because I’ve been meaning to get those checked anyway… but no, I think it’s more that my mind just won’t stay with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… guilty, I suppose that’s the word for it. I keep turning over those last weeks with my sister in my head, like if I look at them from enough angles I’ll find the place where I should have done something different.

I tell myself I should have called more, or listened better when she did talk, or done something actually useful instead of just… well, I don’t know what. But yes, nearly every day since then. At least a few weeks… about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Food just doesn’t really call to me much lately.\n\nI’ll make something small, like toast or a little soup, and then I get distracted by… well, the dishes, or some envelope I meant to open, and suddenly it’s evening and I realize I’ve hardly eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It’s just that eating feels like one more thing I have to remember to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think anyone’s said I’m talking slower… though I suppose I haven’t been talking to that many people lately.\n\nMoving slower—maybe a little? But then I’m 69, so sometimes I wonder what’s just normal creaking and what’s… something else. My sister used to tease me because I’d take forever choosing tomatoes at the store, but that was always me, even before all this.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly. Or done something actually useful.\n\nShe always hated fuss, you know, she’d say, “Don’t hover,” and then I think maybe I took that too literally. I’ll be washing a cup or looking at the phone and suddenly I’m thinking about some afternoon I didn’t call because I was… I don’t even know, sorting coupons or something pointless. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but I still keep telling myself I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Even little things seem to take it out of me now. Like running a simple errand, or getting the papers together for the Cultural Center—those shouldn’t be big tasks, but afterward I feel completely worn out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… it’s like my body is moving through thick mud. I’ll start sorting papers and then notice I’m just sitting there holding the same folder. And then I think, well, maybe I should reorganize the whole drawer while I’m at it, because there are receipts from years ago in there, and my sister always kept her paperwork so neat—\n\nBut yes. Tired. Low energy. More than usual, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I’ll sit down with my budgeting spreadsheet—just the usual household things, nothing complicated—and I’ll find myself staring at the same line like it’s written in another language.\n\nOr I’ll be reading a paragraph for the workshop, and I have to reread it three or four times and still don’t really take in what it says. Then I start thinking maybe I need better lighting, or maybe it’s my glasses, because I’ve been meaning to get those checked anyway… but no, I think it’s more that my mind just won’t stay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, I suppose that’s the word for it.
